---
description: "How one of the ZX Spectrum Next's three AY sound chips works: its sixteen registers and the ports that reach them, the counters that make its three tones, the shift register behind its noise, the mixer register that combines them, the logarithmic volume table, the envelope and its sixteen shapes, the differences between the AY-3-8910 and YM2149 modes, how programs make music with it, and how Klive emulates it."
---

import { Callout } from 'nextra/components'

# ZX Spectrum Next: One AY Chip

Sixteen registers. Write three numbers and the chip plays a chord forever, with no further help from
the CPU. The whole soundtrack of the ZX Spectrum 128K was made from three counters, a random-number
generator and a staircase. How can so little make music?

The AY chip is the sound of the 128K era and of most Spectrum music since: game soundtracks, demo
music, the trackers musicians still write in today. It is a **generator** (the overview's word): once a
program has set it up, it plays by itself, and the CPU is free to run the game. The Next has three of
these chips. This article takes one of them apart; the next one,
TurboSound Next (article 4), puts the three together.

This is article 3 of the series; read
[The Big Picture](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-audio/overview/) first, which introduces
the four kinds of source and the difference between a generator and a level writer. The background page
[How a Computer Makes Sound](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/computer-sound/) explains square
waves and a DAC. Where the chip's output goes next is in
[The Mixer and the Outputs](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-audio/mixer/).

### How this article is organised

- **The chip** ([Part 1](#part-1-the-chip)): the parts of one AY chip, and where its sound comes from.
- **Sixteen registers and two ports** ([Part 2](#part-2-sixteen-registers-and-two-ports)): how a
  program reaches the chip.
- **Tones** ([Part 3](#part-3-tones)): a counter that makes a square wave, and how its period becomes a
  pitch.
- **Noise** ([Part 4](#part-4-noise)): a shift register that makes random bits.
- **The mixer register** ([Part 5](#part-5-the-mixer-register)): tone, noise, both or neither, per
  channel.
- **Volume** ([Part 6](#part-6-volume)): sixteen fixed levels and the table that makes them.
- **The envelope** ([Part 7](#part-7-the-envelope)): a volume that moves by itself, in sixteen shapes.
  If you read only one part, read this one.
- **AY or YM** ([Part 8](#part-8-ay-or-ym)): the two chips the Next can be, and what differs.
- **Making music** ([Part 9](#part-9-making-music)): chords, effects, and bass from the envelope.
- **How Klive does it** ([Part 10](#part-10-how-klive-does-it)): the emulator's chip.

<Callout type="info">
The chip is in **YM2149 mode** (the state after power-on) unless a Part says otherwise. Its counters step
**218,750 times a second** (Part 3). Levels are in the **units of the Next's mixer**: one channel at full
volume adds 255. The waveforms are captured from Klive's emulated Next at 48,000 samples a second, from
programs checked to do what the text says; they show Klive's mix, where the AY chips count up from 0 as
they do on the hardware.
</Callout>

## Part 1: The chip

The chip was designed by General Instrument as the **AY-3-8910**, a programmable sound generator, or
PSG; Yamaha made a compatible chip, the **YM2149**. The 128K used the first; the Next builds both into its
FPGA, and a register chooses which one each of its three chips behaves as (Part 8).

Inside, one chip has a handful of parts, all driven by one clock:

<figure>

On the left, the clock of 218,750 ticks a second feeds three tone generators, one per channel, the noise generator and the envelope generator. The tone generators and the noise generator go into the mixer register R7, which makes one on/off signal per channel. The envelope's level goes to all three channels' amplitude registers, each of which picks a fixed volume or the envelope's; the volume table turns that into a level; the three outputs A, B and C leave the chip on the right." style={{ width: "100%", maxWidth: "700px", height: "auto" }}>

{/* /fig:block */}
<figcaption>One AY chip. Three tone generators and one noise generator make on/off signals; the mixer register picks tone, noise or both for each channel; each channel's volume is fixed or follows the envelope; a table turns the volume into a level; three outputs, A, B and C, go to the mixer.</figcaption>
</figure>

- Three **tone generators**, one per channel, each a counter that makes a square wave at a pitch the
  program sets.
- One **noise generator**, a shift register that makes random bits, shared by the three channels.
- The **mixer register**, which decides for each channel whether it gets its tone, the noise, both, or
  neither.
- An **amplitude** per channel: a fixed volume, or the **envelope**, a volume that rises and falls by
  itself; one envelope generator is shared by the three channels.
- A **volume table**, the chip's DAC, which turns each channel's 4- or 5-bit volume into a level.
- Three **outputs**, channels **A**, **B** and **C**, each 0 to 255 units, which go to the mixer.

Every one of these parts is a small counter or a register. None of them can make a curve: everything the
chip plays is built from on/off signals and steps of volume. That is why the AY sounds the way it does.

## Part 2: Sixteen registers and two ports

The chip has **16 registers**, R0 to R15, each 8 bits wide, though many use fewer:

<figure>
{/* fig:regmap */}
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 304" role="img" aria-label="The sixteen registers in two columns, each with the bits it uses drawn as boxes, bit 7 on the left. R0, R2 and R4, the low 8 bits of the tone periods of channels A, B and C; R1, R3 and R5, their top 4 bits. R6, the noise period, 5 bits. R7, the mixer, 8 bits: the I/O directions in bits 7 and 6, noise off for C, B, A in bits 5 to 3, tone off for C, B, A in bits 2 to 0. R8, R9 and R10, the amplitudes, 5 bits. R11 and R12, the envelope period's low and high bytes. R13, the envelope shape, 4 bits. R14 and R15, the I/O ports A and B, 8 bits each." style={{ width: "100%", maxWidth: "700px", height: "auto" }}>
  <text x="10" y="52" fill="#e8912d" fontSize="12.5" fontWeight="bold">R0</text>
  <text x="48" y="52" fill="currentColor" fontSize="12">tone A, low 8 bits</text>

</svg>
{/* /fig:regmap */}
<figcaption>The sixteen registers, with the bits each one uses. Orange bits make sound; grey outlined bits are the I/O ports and their directions; faint bits do nothing.</figcaption>
</figure>

A program reaches them through two ports, the 128K's own:

1. It writes the register's number to **port `$FFFD`**, the register select port. The value must have bits
   7–5 clear: it is a register number, 0 to 31. Only 0 to 15 exist; a write to a register numbered 16 to 31
   is ignored.
2. It writes the value to **port `$BFFD`**, the data port. The selected register stays selected, so a
   program can write it again without selecting it again.
3. It reads the selected register back from port `$FFFD`. (On the +3's timing, port `$BFFD` reads the same.)

A value written to port `$FFFD` with bit 7 set is not a register number; the Next uses those values to
choose between its three chips (article 4). Values with bits 7–5 anything else but `000` or the chip-select
pattern select nothing.

After a reset, every register is 0 except **R7, which is `$FF`**: all tones and noise switched off. With
every volume at 0 the chip is silent until a program sets it up.

**R14 and R15** are not sound registers: they are the chip's two 8-bit **I/O ports**, A and B. On the 128K,
port A drove the serial connector. On the Next, nothing is connected to them. R7 bits 6 and 7 make each
one an output (1) or an input (0); an input reads `$FF`, as if every pin were pulled up, and an output
reads back the value last written.

## Part 3: Tones

### The clock

The chip runs from the Next's **1.75 MHz** PSG clock, the 28 MHz master clock divided by 16. Inside, the
chip divides it by 8 again, so its counters step **218,750 times a second**. Call one of those steps a
**tick** of the chip.

### A counter that makes a square wave

Each channel's **tone period** is a 12-bit number: the low 8 bits in R0 (channel A), R2 (B) or R4 (C), the
top 4 bits in R1, R3 or R5. The tone generator is a counter that counts ticks:

1. At every tick, the counter goes up by one.
2. When it has counted to the period (it has counted `period` ticks since it last started), it starts
   again from 0, and the channel's square wave **flips**: high becomes low, or low becomes high.
3. So each half of the square wave lasts `period` ticks, and a whole period lasts twice that.

Here is a period of 3, tick by tick:

<figcaption>A tone generator with period 3, worked out tick by tick. The counter goes 0, 1, 2 and starts again, and the square wave flips each time it does: 3 ticks per half, 6 per period.</figcaption>
</figure>

The pitch is therefore

> frequency = 218,750 ÷ (2 × period)

The A of 440 Hz needs a period of 218,750 ÷ 880 = 248.6, so 249, which plays 218,750 ÷ 498 = **439.3 Hz**:
as close as a whole number allows, and too little out of tune to hear. Captured from the machine:

<figcaption>Channel A with period 249 at full volume, captured: a square wave of 439.3 Hz, each half 249 ticks of the chip (1.14 ms), swinging from 0 to 255 units.</figcaption>
</figure>

The 12 bits give periods from 1 to 4,095, and so pitches from 218,750 ÷ 8,190 = **26.7 Hz**, a low growl,
up to 109,375 Hz, far above hearing. A period of 0 plays as 1. The steps between pitches are fine at the
bottom of the range and coarse at the top: 249 and 250 are 1.8 Hz apart, but 10 and 11 are almost 1,000 Hz
apart, so very high notes come out of tune.

The 128K's AY ran from a clock of about 1.77 MHz instead of 1.75 MHz. The same period plays about 1.3 %
lower on the Next, a quarter of a semitone: a 128K tune keeps its tune, a little lower.

## Part 4: Noise

### A shift register

The noise generator makes random bits with a **17-bit shift register**:

<figure>
{/* fig:lfsr */}
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 170" role="img" aria-label="Seventeen boxes in a row, bit 16 on the left and bit 0 on the right. Arrows show every bit moving one place to the right at each step. Bit 0 leaves the register on the right as the noise signal. Bit 0 and bit 2 are taken into an exclusive-or, whose result goes back round to become the new bit 16." style={{ width: "100%", maxWidth: "700px", height: "auto" }}>
  <defs>
    <marker id="ay-lfsr-0" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="9" refY="5" markerWidth="7" markerHeight="7" orient="auto-start-reverse">

      <path d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z" fill="#e8912d" />
    </marker>
  </defs>

  <text x="329" y="110" textAnchor="middle" fill="currentColor" fontSize="11.5">every step: each bit moves one place right →</text>
  <path d="M618,75 L658,75" fill="none" stroke="#e8912d" strokeWidth="1.8" markerEnd="url(#ay-lfsr-1)" strokeLinejoin="round" />
  <text x="622" y="96" fill="#e8912d" fontSize="12" fontWeight="bold">noise</text>
  <rect x="535" y="9" width="64" height="22" rx="11" fill="none" stroke="#e8912d" strokeWidth="1.6" />
  <text x="567" y="25" textAnchor="middle" fill="#e8912d" fontSize="11">excl. or</text>
  <path d="M601,60 L601,31 L587,31" fill="none" stroke="#e8912d" strokeWidth="1.4" strokeLinejoin="round" />
  <path d="M533,60 L533,31 L547,31" fill="none" stroke="#e8912d" strokeWidth="1.4" strokeLinejoin="round" />
  <path d="M535,20 L57,20 L57,56" fill="none" stroke="#e8912d" strokeWidth="1.6" markerEnd="url(#ay-lfsr-1)" strokeLinejoin="round" />
  <text x="74" y="15" fill="#e8912d" fontSize="11.5">the new bit 16</text>
</svg>
{/* /fig:lfsr */}
<figcaption>The noise generator's 17-bit shift register. At every step the bits move one place towards bit 0; the new top bit is bit 0 exclusive-or bit 2; bit 0 is the noise signal.</figcaption>
</figure>

1. At every **noise step**, all 17 bits move one place towards bit 0, and the bit that falls out of bit 0
   is lost.
2. The new bit 16 is **bit 0 exclusive-or bit 2**, taken before the move. (If the register is all zeros,
   as it is after a reset, a 1 is fed in instead, so that it gets going.)
3. **Bit 0 is the noise signal**: on or off, like a tone.

The bits it produces look random, though they are worked out exactly: the pattern repeats only after
**114,681** steps. Each step changes the output with a chance of about one half.

### The noise period

The noise generator's counter runs at half the tone generators' rate, 109,375 steps a second, and the
**noise period** in R6 bits 4–0 (1 to 31; 0 plays as 1) divides it once more: the register steps
109,375 ÷ period times a second. A small period gives a high, hissing noise; a large one a slower,
rougher one, closer to a rumble:

{/* /fig:noise */}
<figcaption>Noise on channel A, captured, 10 ms each. With period 1 the register steps 109,375 times a second and the level changes faster than the samples can follow; with period 31 it steps 3,528 times a second, and the runs of on and off are long enough to see.</figcaption>
</figure>

Even at period 31 the pattern takes 114,681 ÷ 3,528 = 32.5 seconds to repeat, so no ear ever hears it
come round. There is only one noise generator per chip: its period is the same for every channel that uses
it.

## Part 5: The mixer register

**R7**, the mixer register, decides what each channel plays. Bits 0–2 switch the tones of A, B and C, bits
3–5 their noise, and both are **active low**: a 0 switches the source on. Each channel's signal is

> (tone, or tone switched off) and (noise, or noise switched off)

which gives four cases:

- **Tone only** (tone bit 0, noise bit 1): the square wave.
- **Noise only** (tone bit 1, noise bit 0): the noise.
- **Tone and noise** (both 0): on only where the tone *and* the noise are on, so a noise that comes and goes
  at the tone's pitch: a pitched hiss, used for drums and engines.
- **Neither** (both 1): the signal is always on, so the channel plays its **volume as a steady level**.

Captured on channel A, with the same tone (period 109) and noise (period 8) each time:

Neither: a flat line at 255, the channel's volume as a steady level." style={{ width: "100%", maxWidth: "700px", height: "auto" }}>

<figcaption>Channel A with R7 set four ways, captured, 10 ms each. Tone and noise together is the noise only while the tone is high; with neither, the channel holds its volume as a steady level.</figcaption>
</figure>

The last case has two consequences that catch programmers out:

- **A channel is silenced by its volume, not by R7.** Switching off a channel's tone and noise does not
  make it silent: it makes it a steady level, which is heard as a click when it appears and disappears. The
  silent way is volume 0.
- **The steady level is a DAC.** A program that switches off a channel's tone and noise and then rewrites
  its volume many times a second plays a waveform through the volume table: that is how 128K programs
  played sampled drums and speech on the AY, before DAC sound cards.

R7 bits 6 and 7 are not sound: they set the direction of the I/O ports (Part 2).

## Part 6: Volume

Each channel's **amplitude register**, R8 (A), R9 (B) or R10 (C), sets its loudness:

- **Bits 3–0**: a fixed volume, 0 to 15. 0 is silent.
- **Bit 4**: when it is 1, the channel ignores bits 3–0 and follows the envelope (Part 7).

The volume is not the level. It goes through the chip's **volume table**, which turns it into the level that
reaches the mixer. The table is **logarithmic**: each step up multiplies the level by roughly the same
factor, because the ear hears loudness as a ratio: doubling a level sounds like the same step up whether it
goes from 32 to 64 or from 128 to 255. On a straight scale, the top volumes would sound almost alike and the
bottom ones would jump.

The YM2149 has a table of 32 entries, and a fixed volume `v` uses entry 2 × `v` + 1, every other entry. The
AY-3-8910 has a table of 16, one per volume. They are not the same:

<figure>
{/* fig:volume */}
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 330" role="img" aria-label="A chart of the level each fixed volume 0 to 15 gives, in mixer units from 0 to 255. Two lines of points: YM2149 mode, which uses every other entry of its 32-entry table, and AY-3-8910 mode, its own 16 entries. Both start at 0 and end at 255 and curve upwards, rising slowly at first and steeply at the top. The AY line lies above the YM line at every volume from 1 to 14: volume 8 gives 40 in AY mode and 27 in YM mode." style={{ width: "100%", maxWidth: "700px", height: "auto" }}>

{/* /fig:volume */}
<figcaption>The level each fixed volume gives, in mixer units, in YM2149 mode (red) and AY-3-8910 mode (orange). Both reach 255 at volume 15, but AY mode is louder at every volume below it.</figcaption>
</figure>

At volume 15 both give 255. Below it, the AY table is louder: volume 8 gives 40 in AY mode and 27 in YM
mode, volume 1 gives 3 against 2. A 128K tune that uses middle volumes for its accompaniment sounds more
balanced in AY mode, the chip it was written for.

## Part 7: The envelope

An **envelope** is a volume that moves by itself. A channel whose amplitude register has bit 4 set takes its
volume from the chip's one **envelope generator**, which steps up or down at a rate the program sets, in one
of sixteen **shapes**.

### The period and the steps

The **envelope period** is a 16-bit number, R11 (the low byte) and R12 (the high byte). Like a tone counter,
the envelope's counter counts ticks, and every `period` ticks (0 plays as 1) the envelope takes one **step**.
A ramp from silence to full volume, or back, is **32 steps**, so it lasts

> 32 × period ÷ 218,750 seconds

from 146 µs at period 1 to 9.6 seconds at 65,535. A drum hit needs a few milliseconds; a slowly fading note a
second or more.

### The sixteen shapes

R13 bits 3–0 choose the shape, and each bit has a name:

- **ATT** (attack, bit 2): 1, the envelope starts at the bottom and climbs; 0, it starts at the top and falls.
- **CONT** (continue, bit 3): 0, after one ramp it drops to 0 and stays there, whatever the other two bits
  say.
- **HOLD** (bit 0), with CONT set: after one ramp it stops and holds its level.
- **ALT** (alternate, bit 1), with CONT set: the next ramp goes the other way. With HOLD, the envelope holds
  the level at the *opposite* end of where the ramp finished.

Worked through for two shapes:

- **Shape 13**, `1101` (CONT, ATT, HOLD): it starts at the bottom and climbs (ATT); at the top it stops
  (HOLD), and stays at full volume. The sound of a note that swells and then sustains.
- **Shape 14**, `1110` (CONT, ATT, ALT): it climbs; at the top, CONT keeps it going and ALT turns it round, so
  it falls; at the bottom it turns again. A triangle, forever.

All sixteen, captured from the machine with the same period:

<figure>
{/* fig:envgrid */}
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 470" role="img" aria-label="Sixteen small captures in a grid of four by four, ten milliseconds each, one per envelope shape 0 to 15, each labelled with its four bits CONT, ATT, ALT, HOLD. Shapes 0 to 3 fall once and stay silent; shapes 4 to 7 climb once and fall silent; these eight are drawn faint, marked as the same as shape 9 and shape 15. Shape 8 falls again and again, a saw tooth. Shape 9 falls once and stays silent. Shape 10 falls and climbs, a triangle. Shape 11 falls once and jumps to full volume. Shape 12 climbs again and again. Shape 13 climbs once and holds at full volume. Shape 14 climbs and falls, a triangle. Shape 15 climbs once and falls silent." style={{ width: "100%", maxWidth: "700px", height: "auto" }}>

  <text x="10" y="458" fill="currentColor" fontSize="11.5">bits: CONT ATT ALT HOLD (R13 bits 3–0); 10 ms each, a ramp of 2.9 ms</text>
</svg>
{/* /fig:envgrid */}
<figcaption>The sixteen envelope shapes, captured from the machine, 10 ms each with a period of 20 ticks (a ramp of 2.9 ms). Shapes 0 to 3 play as shape 9, and shapes 4 to 7 as shape 15, so there are eight different ones.</figcaption>
</figure>

With CONT clear, ALT and HOLD do nothing, so shapes 0–3 are all "fall once, then silence", the same as shape
9, and shapes 4–7 are all "climb once, then silence", the same as shape 15. The ramps curve instead of
running straight: every step goes through the logarithmic volume table (Part 6), so the level grows by a
factor at each step, which the ear hears as an even climb. Sixteen shapes, eight sounds:

- Fall once and stay silent (0–3, 9), or climb once and fall silent (4–7, 15): a plucked or struck note.
- Fall, again and again (8), or climb, again and again (12): a saw tooth.
- Fall and climb (10), or climb and fall (14), forever: a triangle.
- Fall once and jump to full volume (11), or climb once and stay there (13).

**Writing R13 restarts the envelope**, even with the value it already holds: it goes back to the start of its
shape at once. That is how a program strikes a new note with the same shape: one write per note.

### 32 steps or 16

In YM2149 mode, the envelope's 32 steps go through the 32-entry table, each one to its own level. In
AY-3-8910 mode the table has 16 entries, so the envelope uses only its top four bits: each level lasts two
steps. The ramp takes exactly as long, but it climbs in 16 coarser steps:

<figure>
{/* fig:climb */}

AY-3-8910 mode: a coarser staircase of 16 steps, each twice as long, reaching 255 at the same moment." style={{ width: "100%", maxWidth: "700px", height: "auto" }}>

<figcaption>Shape 13 (climb and hold) with a period of 60 ticks, captured in both modes. In YM2149 mode it climbs in 32 steps of 274 µs; in AY-3-8910 mode in 16 steps of twice that. Both reach full volume at the same moment, 8.8 ms after the write.</figcaption>
</figure>

The YM's ramp shows fewer than 32 distinct levels at its bottom, because its table's lowest entries come in
equal pairs (1, 1, 2, 2, 3, 3): there are 29 different levels in all.

## Part 8: AY or YM

The **Peripheral 2 Setting register, NextReg `$06`**, bits 1–0, set what all three of the Next's chips are:

- `00` (the power-on value): **YM2149**.
- `01`: **AY-3-8910**.
- `10`: the register list calls it "ZXN-8950", but the chips look only at bit 0, so it behaves exactly as
  YM2149. There is no third kind of chip.
- `11`: **holds all three chips in reset**: silent, registers cleared, writes ignored, until the mode is
  changed again.

What a program can see change between the two modes:

- **The volume table** (Part 6): AY mode is louder below volume 15.
- **The envelope's resolution** (Part 7): 16 levels in AY mode, 32 in YM mode, over the same time.
- **The bits a register reads back.** In YM mode, every register reads back all 8 bits as written. In AY mode,
  the bits a register does not use read as 0: R1, R3, R5 and R13 keep their low 4 bits, R6, R8, R9 and R10
  their low 5 bits; the others read whole.
- **Registers 16 to 31**, which do not exist: in YM mode they read `$FF`; in AY mode they read register
  number − 16, as if the top bit of the number were ignored. Writes to them are dropped in both modes.

A program that detects which chip it is on writes a value with the top bits set to R1, say, and reads it
back: whole in YM mode, its low 4 bits in AY mode.

One more read is the Next's own: **port `$BFF5`** (an address that the data port `$BFFD` also answers) reads
the selected chip's identity in bits 7–6 and the selected register's number in bits 4–0, for software that
must know which chip and register it last selected (article 4).

## Part 9: Making music

### A chord

Three channels, three notes. C, E and G above middle C are periods 209, 166 and 140 (523.3, 658.9 and
781.3 Hz). Set once, they play until something changes them:

{/* /fig:chord */}
<figcaption>A C major chord, captured: channel A plays C (523.3 Hz), B plays E (658.9 Hz), C plays G (781.3 Hz), each alone at full volume, and the three added together, the sum a chip in mono gives both sides (article 4).</figcaption>
</figure>

A music player does not touch the chip while a note sounds. Once a frame, 50 times a second, it moves on in
the tune: when a note ends, it writes the next note's period and volume, or restarts the envelope with R13.
A few register writes per frame is the whole cost of the music to the CPU.

### Effects by rewriting registers

The once-a-frame player is also how the classic effects are made, by changing a register a little every
frame:

- **Vibrato**: the period moves up and down by one or two around the note, a wobble in pitch.
- **Tremolo**: the volume moves up and down, a wobble in loudness.
- **Arpeggio**: one channel cycles through the notes of a chord, a new one every frame or two, so a single
  voice sounds like a chord. Three channels can then play six or nine "notes", the chip-music sound.
- **Slides**: the period changes by a fixed amount every frame, for pitch bends and laser sounds.

### Bass from the envelope

The envelope does not have to be slow. With a short period, a repeating shape is itself a waveform: shape 8,
a falling saw tooth, repeats every 32 steps, so with a period of 14 it plays 218,750 ÷ (32 × 14) =
**488.3 Hz**. Shape 10, a triangle, takes 64 steps for one cycle, so a period of 7 gives the same pitch:

<figcaption>The envelope as a waveform, captured: shape 8 with a period of 14 ticks, a saw tooth, and shape 10 with a period of 7, a triangle, both at 488.3 Hz. Neither has a tone switched on: each is a steady channel whose volume follows the envelope.</figcaption>
</figure>

These waves sound very different from the square tones, and much richer, which made the "buzzer" bass a
favourite of AY musicians. The price is that there is only one envelope per chip: a channel that plays the
bass takes the envelope away from the other two. Musicians often switch on the channel's tone as well, at a
pitch related to the envelope's: the mixer register then lets the envelope's wave through only while the tone
is high, which gives yet another colour.

## Part 10: How Klive does it

Klive's Next core is a C program compiled to WebAssembly; its three AY chips are one module, written to
follow the hardware step for step.

### Ticks, brought up to date when needed

Each chip keeps the same state as the hardware: its sixteen registers, its three tone counters and their
outputs, the noise generator's counter and shift register, and the envelope's counter, level and direction.
One **tick** of the emulated chip is one tick of the real one, every 128 master clocks. The chip is not ticked
in step with the CPU: it is brought up to date when something needs it, a write to one of its ports or the
moment a sample of sound is due, by running all the ticks that have passed since the last time.

### Samples

The chip's outputs change at its ticks, 218,750 times a second, far more often than a sample is taken.
For the audio jack and the speaker, Klive averages each channel's level over the time a sample covers, as the
smoothing after the real converter would; for HDMI it takes the level at the sample's moment, as HDMI does.
The levels go to the mixer as the hardware's are, from 0 upwards: unlike the beeper, Klive does not remove the
AY's constant level (the mixer article's part
[How Klive does it](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-audio/mixer/#part-7-how-klive-does-it)
explains why).

### Where to read the code

| What | Where |
|---|---|
| The three chips: registers, ports, counters, noise, envelope, volume tables, AY and YM modes | `src/emu/machines/zxNext/wasm/zxnext/zxnext-psg.c` |
| Ports `$FFFD`, `$BFFD` and `$BFF5` | `zxnext-ports.c` in the same folder |
| Where the outputs are added | `zxnext-audio-mixer.c` in the same folder |

## Summary

- One AY chip has **three tone generators**, **one noise generator**, a **mixer register**, a volume or the
  **envelope** per channel and a logarithmic **volume table**: three outputs, A, B and C, each 0 to 255 units.
- **16 registers**, reached through **port `$FFFD`** (select, bits 7–5 clear) and **port `$BFFD`** (data);
  reads come from port `$FFFD`. After a reset all are 0 except R7, `$FF`.
- The counters step **218,750 times a second** (1.75 MHz ÷ 8). A tone's 12-bit **period** gives
  218,750 ÷ (2 × period): 249 is A at 439.3 Hz. Period 0 plays as 1.
- **Noise** is a 17-bit shift register fed with bit 0 exclusive-or bit 2, stepping 109,375 ÷ period times a
  second (R6, 1–31).
- **R7** switches each channel's tone and noise, active low; a channel with neither plays its **volume as a
  steady level**, so silence is volume 0.
- **R8–R10**: a fixed volume 0–15 (YM entry 2v + 1, or the AY's own table, louder below 15) or bit 4 for the
  envelope.
- The **envelope** steps every period ticks (R11–R12, 16 bits), 32 steps a ramp; **R13** chooses one of 16
  shapes (CONT, ATT, ALT, HOLD), eight of them different, and a write restarts it.
- **NextReg `$06` bits 1–0**: YM2149 (`00`, `10`), AY-3-8910 (`01`: 16-level envelope, read masks), or all
  chips held in reset (`11`).
- Music: a few writes per frame play three voices; vibrato, tremolo and arpeggios rewrite registers; a short
  envelope period makes a **saw-tooth or triangle bass**.
- **Klive** ticks each chip as the hardware does, brought up to date when a port or a sample needs it, and
  averages each sample's level.
